(8.6.3)

\phi\equiv a^{0}a^{1}_{2}\tau^{\prime}_{\phi}a^{1}\tau^{\prime\prime}_{\phi}a^{0}_{2},

.

Claim 8.7
.

Let
\phi
be a conformal model of
(U,{\sim})
of the form (
8.6.3
).
Then:

(1)

\pi^{\prime}_{1}
and
\pi^{\prime}_{2}
are subwords of
\tau^{\prime}_{\phi}
and
|\pi^{\prime}_{1}|+|\pi^{\prime}_{2}|=|\tau^{\prime}_{\phi}|
,

(2)

\pi^{\prime\prime}_{1}
and
\pi^{\prime\prime}_{2}
are subwords of
\tau^{\prime\prime}_{\phi}
and
|\pi^{\prime\prime}_{1}|+|\pi^{\prime\prime}_{2}|=|\tau^{\prime\prime}_{\phi}|
.

(3)

For every
u\in\alpha(M_{1})
and every
v\in\alpha(M_{2})
, either

\phi(u)
and
\phi(v)
are on different sides of
\phi(a)
, or
there are on the same side of
\phi(a)
and then
\phi(u)
has the chords
\phi(v)
and
\phi(a)
on different sides.

Proof.

See Figure
8.9
for an illustration.
The first two statements follow from the fact that
\phi\|P^{*}_{i}\equiv\phi_{P_{i}}
for
i\in[2]
.

To show (
3
), suppose
\phi(u)
and
\phi(v)
are on the same side of
\phi(a)
,
but the chord
\phi(v)
has the chords
\phi(u)
and
\phi(a)
on different sides.
Then,
\phi(v)
intersects some chord from
\phi(P_{1})
as
(P_{1},{\sim})
is connected.
However, this is not possible as
v\parallel P_{1}
.
So, suppose
\phi(u)
,
\phi(v)
, and
\phi(a)
are in series, that is, no chord from
\phi(a)
,
\phi(u)
, and
\phi(v)

has the remaining two chords on its different sides.
Note that there is
v^{\prime}\in\alpha(M_{2})
such that

v
and
v^{\prime}
are in the same component
(D,{\sim})
of
(\alpha(M_{2}),{\sim})

and
v^{\prime}
is adjacent to some vertex
y\in M_{2}
.
Since
\phi(y)
intersects both the chords
\phi(u)
and
\phi(v^{\prime})
and
u\parallel D
,
we deduce that the chord
\phi(v^{\prime})
has the chords
\phi(a)
and
\phi(u)
on its different sides.
However, we already have shown that such a case is not possible.

We say that

and

and

occur in different orders in the words
\tau^{\prime}_{\phi_{1}}
and

– see Figure

We introduce the notion of being mixed in

and
\tau^{\prime\prime}_{\phi_{2}}

Clearly, if
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
are non-equivalent,
there are vertices

and

and

and

and

are mixed in
\tau^{\prime}_{\phi_{1}}
and
\tau^{\prime}_{\phi_{2}}
.
We claim that
x\in M_{1}
and

.
We can not have

and

as we have

.

.(

) we can not have

.
Thus, we have

, which means that

and

are also mixed in
\tau^{\prime\prime}_{\phi_{1}}
and
\tau^{\prime\prime}_{\phi_{2}}
.

and

are mixed
if

and

are mixed in
\tau^{\prime}_{\phi_{1}}
and
\tau^{\prime}_{\phi_{2}}
and in
\tau^{\prime\prime}_{\phi_{1}}
and
\tau^{\prime\prime}_{\phi_{2}}
.
We claim that:

(8.7.1)

\begin{array}[]{c}\text{If $x\in M_{1}$ and $y\in M_{2}$ are mixed, then }\\
\{x,y\}\sim\alpha(M_{1})\text{ and }\{x,y\}\parallel\alpha(M_{2}).\end{array}

We prove
\{x,y\}\sim\alpha(M_{1})
.
Clearly,
y\sim\alpha(M_{1})
and
x\parallel\alpha(M_{2})
by the properties of the probes
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
.
Suppose there is
u\in\alpha(M_{1})
such that
x\parallel u
.
If this is the case, the relative position of
\phi(x)
and
\phi(u)
is the same
in any conformal model
\phi
of
(U,{\sim})
of the form (
8.6.3
).
Since
\phi_{i}(y)
intersects
\phi_{i}(u)
for every
i\in[2]
,
x
and
y
can not be mixed.
The second statement of (
8.7.1
) is proved similarly.
Now, note that
(C_{1},\sim)
is a permutation subgraph of
(U,{\sim})
as
a\sim C_{1}
.
Hence, if
x\in M_{1}
is mixed with

and

for some

,
then
x_{1}
is also mixed with

.
Similarly, if
x\in M_{1}
is mixed with
y\in M_{2}
and

for some
y_{1}\in M_{2}
,
then

is also mixed with
x
.
Now, let

that is,

contains all the elements in

that are mixed with some other element in
C_{1}
.
Note that
|W|\geqslant 2
as there are at least two elements that are mixed.
Moreover,

and

by the observations given above.

and

by (
8.7.1
),

is a non-trivial module in
(U,{\sim})
, which yields a contradiction.

Before we prove Properties

and

of

.

Claim 8.8
.

Suppose
K
is as defined in Definition
6.5
.

(1)

If
M_{i}
is parallel, then for every
v,w\in M_{i}
:

vKw\quad\iff\quad\begin{array}[]{c}\vskip 1.0pt\text{either $\{v,w\}\subseteq\mathsf{left}(u)$ or $\{v,w\}\subseteq\mathsf{right}(u)$,}\\
\text{for every $u\in U\smallsetminus M_{i}$ such that $u\parallel M_{i}$.}\end{array}

(2)

If
M_{i}
is serial, then for every
v,w\in M_{i}
:

vKw\quad\iff\quad\begin{array}[]{c}\vskip 1.0pt\{\mathsf{left}(v)\cap(U\smallsetminus M_{i}),\mathsf{right}(v)\cap(U\smallsetminus M_{i})\}=\\
\{\mathsf{left}(w)\cap(U\smallsetminus M_{i}),\mathsf{right}(w)\cap(U\smallsetminus M_{i})\}.\end{array}

Proof.

To prove (
1
) we need to show, for every
v,w\in M_{i}
,
the equivalence between the following two statements:

(8.8.1)

\{v,w\}\subseteq\mathsf{left}(u)\text{ or }\{v,w\}\subseteq\mathsf{right}(u)\text{ for every $u\in U\smallsetminus M_{i}$ such that $u\parallel M_{i}$}.

(8.8.2)

\{v,w\}\subseteq\mathsf{left}(u)\text{ or }\{v,w\}\subseteq\mathsf{right}(u)\text{ for every $u\in Q\smallsetminus M_{i}$ such that $u\parallel M_{i}$}.

We need to show that (

) implies (

), as the inverse implication is obvious.
Suppose (

) holds,
but there is

such that

are on different sides of

– see Figure

to the left.

for some

.
Let

.
In particular,

and

.

.
Now, let

.
Note that

.

.

m_{1}\in M_{j}
and
m_{2}\in M_{j^{\prime}}
for some
j\neq j^{\prime}
distinct from
i
.
Let
u_{1},u_{2}\in U
be such that
u_{1}\in M_{j}
and
u_{2}\in M_{j^{\prime}}
.
Note that the chord
\phi(u_{1})
is either between
w^{1}
and
v^{0}
or between
w^{0}
and
v^{1}
in
\phi
as there is
y\in Q\smallsetminus(M_{j}\cup M_{i})
such that
y\sim\{u_{1},m_{1}\}
and either
y\sim\{v,w\}
or
y\parallel\{v,w\}
.
Similarly, the chord
\phi(u_{2})
is either between
v^{1}
and
w^{1}
or between
v^{0}
and
w^{0}
– see Figure
8.10
to the right.
In any case,
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
contradict (
8.8.3
).
The other cases are proved analogously.
∎

The next lemma proves Properties
(P1):
and
(P2):
of the set
K(Q)
.

Lemma 8.9
.

Let
K_{1},\ldots,K_{k}
be the sets in
K(Q)
and let
R=\{r_{1},\ldots,r_{k}\}
be the set such that
r_{i}\in K_{i}
for
i\in[k]
.
Then:

(1)

The graph
(R,{\sim})
has exactly two conformal models,
\phi^{0}_{R}
and
\phi^{1}_{R}
, one being the reflection of the other.

(2)

For every conformal model
\phi
of
(Q,{\sim})
and every
j\in[k]
,
the set
K_{j}
induces a consistent permutation model of
(K_{j},{\sim})
in
\phi
.

Proof.

Note that
n\leqslant k
.
Without loss of generality we assume that
U=\{r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}\}
and

r_{i}\in M_{i}
for
i\in[n]
.
Let
R_{j}=\{r_{1},\ldots,r_{j}\}
for every
j\in[k]
.
By Lemma
8.4
,
(U,{\sim})
has two conformal models,

\phi^{0}_{U}
and its reflection
\phi^{1}_{U}
.
Our proof is based on the following statement:

(8.9.1)

\begin{array}[]{c}\text{For every $j=\{|U|,\ldots,k\}$ and every $m\in\{0,1\}$ there is a unique}\\
\text{conformal model $\phi^{m}_{j}$ of $(R_{j},{\sim})$ such that $\phi^{m}_{j}\|U^{*}\equiv\phi^{m}_{U}$.}\end{array}

Clearly, statement (
1
) follows from statement (
8.9.1
) for
j=k
.

We prove the statement by induction on
j
.
For
j=|U|=n
statement (
8.9.1
) is satisfied as
R_{n}=U
.
Suppose (
8.9.1
) holds for
j=l-1
for some
l>|U|
.
Our goal is to prove it for
j=l
.
Suppose
r_{l}
is in the module
M_{i}
for some
i\in[k]
.
From the inductive hypothesis, there is a unique extension
\phi^{m}_{l-1}
of
\phi^{m}_{U}
on the set
R_{l-1}
.
Suppose for a contradiction that there are two non-equivalent conformal models of
(R_{l},{\sim})
extending
\phi^{m}_{l-1}
by the chord for
r_{l}
.
Equivalently,
there is a circular word
\phi
extending
\phi^{m}_{l-1}
by the letters
x^{0},x^{1},y^{0},y^{1}

such that
\phi^{\prime}\equiv\phi\|(R_{l-1}\cup\{x\})^{*}
and
\phi^{\prime\prime}\equiv\phi\|(R_{l-1}\cup\{y\})^{*}
are two non-equivalent conformal models of
(R_{l},{\sim})

after replacing
x^{0},x^{1}
by
r^{0}_{l},r^{1}_{l}
in
\phi^{\prime}
and
y^{0},y^{1}
by
r^{0}_{l},r^{1}_{l}
in
\phi^{\prime\prime}
, respectively.
Note that for every
r\in R_{l-1}
the circular word
\phi
satisfies the following properties:

•

r\in\mathsf{left}(r_{l})\iff\text{$\phi(r)$ is on the left side of $\phi(x)$ and $\phi(y)$}
,

•

r\in\mathsf{right}(r_{l})\iff\text{$\phi(r)$ is on the right side of $\phi(x)$ and $\phi(y)$}
,

•

r_{l}\in\mathsf{left}(r)\iff\text{$\phi(x)$ and $\phi(y)$ are on the left side of $\phi(r)$}
,

•

r_{l}\in\mathsf{right}(r)\iff\text{$\phi(x)$ and $\phi(y)$ are on the right side of $\phi(r)$}
.

We consider two cases depending on whether the chords
\phi(x)
and
\phi(y)
intersect in
\phi
.

Suppose
\phi(x)
and
\phi(y)
do not intersect.
Suppose we have
\phi\|\{x,y\}^{*}\equiv x^{0}x^{1}y^{1}y^{0}
– see Figure
8.11
to the left.
Since
\phi^{\prime}
and
\phi^{\prime\prime}
are non-equivalent, there is
r\in R_{l-1}

such that
\phi(r)
has one of its ends between
x^{1}
and
y^{1}
or between
y^{0}
and
x^{0}
.
Since the chord
\phi(r)
can not intersect both
\phi(x)
and
\phi(y)
,

\phi(r)
must be on the right side of
\phi(x)
and the left side of
\phi(y)
.
However, this contradicts the properties of
\phi
listed above.
So, suppose we have
\phi\|\{x,y\}^{*}\equiv x^{0}x^{1}y^{0}y^{1}
– see Figure
8.11
in the middle.
Let
r\in R_{l-1}
be such that
r\parallel r_{l}
.
The chord
\phi(r)
must lie on the right side of
\phi(x)
and the right side of
\phi(y)
as
any other placement of
\phi(r)
would contradict the properties of
\phi
.
For the same reason,
\phi(r)
can not have
\phi(x)
and
\phi(y)
on its different sides.
Hence,
\phi(r)
has both its ends either between
x^{1}
and
y^{0}
or
between
y^{1}
and
x^{0}
.
Note that
r
can not belong to
M_{i}
as otherwise the chord
\phi(u)
, where
u\in U\smallsetminus M_{i}
is such that
u\sim M_{i}
,
could not intersect
\phi(r)
,
\phi(x)
, and
\phi(y)
at the same time.
Let
P
be a path in
(R_{l},{\sim})
joining

and
r_{l}
with all inner vertices in
U
.
Note that there must be a vertex
u\in U
in the path

such that
\phi(u)
has
\phi(x)
and
\phi(y)
on its different sides.
This contradicts the properties of
\phi
.
The remaining cases are proved similarly.

.

form

.
So, we have

.
In particular,

must be serial as

.
Since for every

such that

the chord

and

, we have

for every

.(

).
However, this can not be the case as

-relation.

To prove statement (

)
assume that

.

Suppose

.

, where

.

.

, enumerated such that

.

.

.

.

.

.

Suppose

, where

.
Assume that

is such that

for some

.
Suppose

are the children of

contained in

, enumerated such that

\phi\|(\{x\}\cup L_{1}\cup\ldots\cup L_{p})^{*}\equiv x^{0}\lambda^{0}_{1}\ldots\lambda^{0}_{p}x^{1}\lambda^{1}_{p}\ldots\lambda^{1}_{0},

where

asserts that

,

9.
Conformal models for parallel case - appendix

Properties
(T1):
–
(T4):

of the PQS-tree
\mathbb{T}
of
G
were shown by Hsu
[
14
]
.
For the sake of completeness, their proofs are also provided below.

9.1.
Properties of
\mathbb{T}

We start this section with the claim which proves Property
(T1):
of the graph
\mathbb{T}
.
We recall that at this stage
\mathbb{T}
contains only P-nodes and Q-nodes.

Claim 9.1
.

The following statements hold:

(1)

For every Q-node
Q
in
\mathbb{T}
and every two P-nodes
P_{1},P_{2}\in N_{\mathbb{T}}(Q)
there is a vertex
v\in Q
that separates
the components from
P_{1}\smallsetminus\{Q\}
and the components from
P_{2}\smallsetminus\{Q\}
.

(2)

The bipartite graph
\mathbb{T}
is a tree.

Proof.

Let
Q
be a Q-node in
\mathbb{T}
and let
P_{1},P_{2}
be two different P-nodes adjacent to
Q
in
\mathbb{T}
.
It means that
Q\in P_{1}
and
Q\in P_{2}
.
Since
P_{1},P_{2}
are different maximal subsets of pairwise neighbouring components from
\mathcal{Q}
,
there is a component
Q_{1}\in P_{1}\smallsetminus P_{2}
and a component
Q_{2}\in P_{2}\smallsetminus P_{1}
such that
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
are separated by some
v\in V\smallsetminus(Q_{1}\cup Q_{2})
.
Suppose
Q_{1}\subseteq\mathsf{left}(v)
and
Q_{2}\subseteq\mathsf{right}(v)
.
Note that
v\in Q
.
Otherwise, depending on whether
Q\subseteq\mathsf{right}(v)
or whether
Q\subseteq\mathsf{left}(v)
,

v
separates either
Q
and
Q_{1}
or
Q
and
Q_{2}
, and either
P_{1}
or
P_{2}
is not a P-node of
\mathbb{T}
.
Then, the components from
P_{1}\smallsetminus\{Q\}
are on the left side of
v

and the components from
P_{2}\smallsetminus\{Q\}
are on the right side of
v

as otherwise
P_{1}
or
P_{2}
is not a P-node of
\mathbb{T}
.
This proves (
1
).

Now, we show that
\mathbb{T}
is a tree.
First we prove that
\mathbb{T}
contains no cycles.
Suppose that
Q_{1}P_{1}\ldots Q_{k}P_{k}
is a cycle in
\mathbb{T}
, for some
k\geqslant 2
.
Since
P_{1},P_{k}
are neighbors of
Q_{1}
in
\mathbb{T}
, there
is
v\in Q_{1}
that separates the components in
P_{1}\smallsetminus\{Q_{1}\}

and the components in
P_{k}\smallsetminus\{Q_{1}\}
.
So,
v
separates
Q_{2}
and
Q_{k}
.
In particular,
Q_{2}
and
Q_{k}
can not be in a same P-node,
and hence
k\geqslant 3
.
Since
Q_{2}
and
Q_{k}
are separated by
v
,
there is
i\in[2,k-1]
such that
Q_{i}
and
Q_{i+1}
are also separated by
v
.
So,
Q_{i}
and
Q_{i+1}
are not contained in a same P-node,
which is not the case as
P_{i}
contains both
Q_{i}
and
Q_{i+1}
.
This proves that
\mathbb{T}
is a forest.
To complete the proof we show that
\mathbb{T}
is connected.
Suppose
\mathbb{T}
is not connected and suppose
\phi
is any conformal model of
G_{ov}
.
Then, there are vertices
u
and
v
such that
u^{*}
and
v^{*}
are next to each other in
\phi
,

u^{*}\in\{u^{0},u^{1}\}
,
v^{*}\in\{v^{0},v^{1}\}
,
u\in Q_{u}
,
v\in Q_{v}
, and
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
are components from
\mathcal{Q}
from different connected components of the graph
\mathbb{T}
.
By the choice of
u
and
v
, there is no vertex in
V\smallsetminus(Q_{u}\cup Q_{v})

that separates
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
.
So,
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
are contained in some P-node in
\mathbb{T}
,
which contradicts that
Q_{u}
and
Q_{v}
are in different connected components of
\mathbb{T}
.
∎

The next claim shows Property
(T2):
.

Claim 9.2
.

Suppose
Q
is a Q-node in
\mathbb{T}
,
P
is a P-node adjacent to
Q
in
\mathbb{T}
, and
v
is a vertex in
Q
.
Then, either
V_{\mathbb{T}-Q}(P)\subseteq\mathsf{left}(v)
or
V_{\mathbb{T}-Q}(P)\subseteq\mathsf{right}(v)
.

Proof.

Let
F_{P}
be a connected component of
\mathbb{T}\smallsetminus Q
containing
P
.
Suppose there are two components
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in F_{P}

such that
Q_{1}\subseteq\mathsf{left}(v)
and
Q_{2}\subseteq\mathsf{right}(v)
.
Let
p
be a path between
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
in
F_{P}
.
Clearly, there are three consecutive elements
Q^{\prime}_{1}P^{\prime}Q^{\prime}_{2}
on the path
p

such that
Q^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathsf{left}(v)
and
Q^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathsf{right}(v)
.
Thus,
Q^{\prime}_{1}
and
Q^{\prime}_{2}
are separated by
v
,
which contradicts
Q^{\prime}_{1},Q^{\prime}_{2}\in P^{\prime}
.
∎

Finally, Claim
9.3
shows Properties
(T3):
and
(T4):
.

Claim 9.3
.

Let
\phi
be a conformal model of
G_{ov}
.

(1)

For every Q-node
Q
and every
P\in N_{\mathbb{T}}(Q)
the set
V^{*}_{\mathbb{T}-Q}(P)
is contiguous in
\phi
.
Moreover, for every two distinct
P,P^{\prime}\in N_{\mathbb{T}}(Q)
there is
v\in Q

such that
\phi(v)
separates
\phi|V^{*}_{\mathbb{T}-Q}(P)

and
\phi|V^{*}_{\mathbb{T}-Q}(P^{\prime})
.

(2)

For every P-node
P
and every
Q\in N_{\mathbb{T}}(P)
the set
V^{*}_{\mathbb{T}-P}(Q)
is contiguous in
\phi
.

Proof.

Statement (
1
) follows from
Claim
9.2
and Claim
9.1
.(
1
).

Next, we prove statement (
2
).
Since
P
is a maximal subset of
\mathcal{Q}
containing pairwise neighbouring components,
the set
Q^{*}
is contiguous in the circular word
\phi\|(\bigcup P)^{*}
.
Now, statement (
1
) applied to the neighbours of the component
Q
different than
P
proves that
V^{*}_{\mathbb{T}-P}(Q)
is a contiguous subword of
\phi
.
∎

9.2.
The properties of the set
K(Q)
, where
Q
is a serial non-permutation component

In this subsection we show properties
(PS1):
and
(PS2):
of the set
K(Q)
,
where
Q
is a serial non-permutation component of
G_{ov}
and
K(Q)
is as defined in Definition
6.14
.
We assume
M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}
are the children of
Q
.

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
K_{1},\ldots,K_{k}
be the members of
K(Q)
for some
k\leqslant n
and let

R=\{r_{1},\ldots,r_{k}\}
be such that
r_{i}\in K_{i}
for every
i\in[k]
.
Then:

(1)

There are two conformal models of
(R,{\sim})
,
\phi^{0}_{R}
and its reflection
\phi^{1}_{R}
,
such that for every extended conformal model
\phi_{Q}
of
(Q,{\sim})

we have either
\phi_{Q}\|R^{*}=\phi^{0}_{R}
or
\phi_{Q}\|R^{*}=\phi^{1}_{R}
.

(2)

For every extended conformal model
\phi_{Q}
of
(Q,{\sim})
and every
K\in K(Q)
the set
K
induces a consistent permutation model in
\phi^{Q}\equiv\phi\|Q^{*}
.
Moreover, if
K
is the union of at least two children of
Q
, the set
K
induces a consistent permutation model also in
\phi_{Q}
.

Proof.

We assume that
M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}
are enumerated such that
r_{i}\in M_{i}
for every
i\in[k]

(note that
k\leqslant n
).
For
i\in[k]
, let
R_{i}=\{r_{1},\ldots,r_{i}\}
; note that
(R_{i},{\sim})
is a clique for every
i\in[k]
.
We claim that for every
i\in[2,k]
there exist two models of
(R_{i},{\sim})
,
\phi^{0}_{i}

and its reflection
\phi^{1}_{i}
, such that for every extended conformal model
\phi_{Q}
of
(Q,{\sim})
,
either

(9.4.1)

\phi_{Q}\|R^{*}_{i}\equiv\phi^{0}_{i}\quad\text{or}\quad\phi_{Q}\|R^{*}_{i}\equiv\phi^{1}_{i}.

Then, statement (
1
) follows from statement (
9.4.1
) for
i=k
.
We prove (
9.4.1
) by induction on
i
.
Note that
(R_{2},{\sim})
has two conformal models,

\phi^{0}_{2}\equiv r^{0}_{1}r^{0}_{2}r^{1}_{1}r^{1}_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\phi^{1}_{2}\equiv r^{0}_{1}r^{1}_{2}r^{1}_{1}r^{0}_{2},

and
\phi^{0}_{2}
is the reflection of
\phi^{1}_{2}
.
So, statement (
9.4.1
) holds for
i=2
.

Let
j\in[3,k]
.
Suppose (
9.4.1
) holds for all
i\in[2,j-1]
.
To show (
9.4.1
) for
j
it suffices to prove there is a unique extension
\phi^{0}_{j}
of
\phi^{0}_{j-1}
on the set
R_{j}
such that
\phi_{Q}\|R^{*}_{j}\equiv\phi^{0}_{j}
holds for every extended conformal model
\phi_{Q}
of
(Q,{\sim})
such that
\phi_{Q}\|R^{*}_{j-1}\equiv\phi^{0}_{j-1}
.
Suppose for a contradiction that there are two extended conformal models of
(Q,{\sim})
, say
\phi_{Q}
and
\phi^{\prime}_{Q}
,
such that
\phi_{Q}\|R^{*}_{j-1}\equiv\phi^{\prime}_{Q}\|R^{*}_{j-1}\equiv\phi^{0}_{j-1}
and
\phi_{Q}\|R^{*}_{j}\not\equiv\phi^{\prime}_{Q}\|R_{j}
.
That is, the chords
\phi_{Q}(r_{j})
and
\phi^{\prime}_{Q}(r_{j})
extend
\phi^{0}_{j-1}
into two non-equivalent models of
(R_{j},{\sim})
.

r^{0}_{q}
r^{1}_{q}
r^{0}_{p}
r^{1}_{p}
\phi_{Q}(r^{1}_{j})
\phi_{Q}(r^{0}_{j})
\phi^{\prime}_{Q}(r^{0}_{j})
\phi^{\prime}_{Q}(r^{1}_{j})
r^{0}_{q}
r^{1}_{q}
r^{0}_{p}
r^{1}_{p}
\phi_{Q}(r^{0}_{j})
\phi_{Q}(r^{1}_{j})
\phi^{\prime}_{Q}(r^{1}_{j})
\phi^{\prime}_{Q}(r^{0}_{j})

Figure 9.1.

It means that there are two different vertices
r_{q},r_{p}\in R_{j-1}
such that

\phi^{\prime}_{Q}\|\{r_{q},r_{p}\}^{*}\equiv\phi_{Q}\|\{r_{q},r_{p}\}^{*}\equiv r_{p}^{0}r_{q}^{0}r_{p}^{1}r_{q}^{1},

but the chords
\phi_{Q}(r_{j})
and
\phi^{\prime}_{Q}(r_{j})

have its endpoints in different sections
r^{0}_{p}r^{0}_{q}
,
r^{0}_{q}r^{1}_{p}
,
r^{1}_{p}r^{1}_{q}
,
r^{1}_{q}r^{0}_{q}
of the circular word

r_{p}^{0}r_{q}^{0}r_{p}^{1}r_{q}^{1}
– see Figure
9.1
for an illustration.

First, suppose the case

\phi_{Q}\|\{r_{q},r_{p},r_{j}\}^{*}\equiv r_{p}^{0}r_{j}^{0}r^{0}_{q}r_{p}^{1}r_{j}^{1}r_{q}^{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\phi^{\prime}_{Q}\|\{r_{q},r_{p},r_{j}\}^{*}\equiv r_{p}^{0}r_{q}^{0}r_{j}^{0}r_{p}^{1}r_{q}^{1}r_{j}^{1},

see Figure
9.1
to the left.
We claim that
\mathsf{inside}(M_{q})\neq\emptyset
.
Suppose to the contrary that there is
P\in N_{\mathbb{T}}(Q)
such that
P\in\mathsf{inside}(M_{q})
.
Assume that
P\in\mathsf{left}(r_{p})
.
Then, in
\phi_{Q}
the letter
P
is on the left side of
\phi_{Q}(r_{j})

and in the model
\phi^{\prime}_{Q}
the letter
P
is on the right side of
\phi^{\prime}_{Q}(r_{j})
,
which can not be the case.
The case when
P\in\mathsf{right}(r_{p})
is proven analogously.
Using similar arguments we show that
\mathsf{inside}(M_{j})=\emptyset
.
To complete the proof in this case, we show that for every
P\in N_{\mathbb{T}}(Q)
,

P\in\mathsf{left}(r_{q})\iff P\in\mathsf{left}(r_{j}),

which shows
r_{j}Kr_{q}
and contradicts the fact that
r_{j}
and
r_{q}
are from two different sets in
K(Q)
.
Suppose that
P\in\mathsf{left}(r_{q})
and
P\in\mathsf{right}(r_{j})
.
Then
P
is between
r^{1}_{j}
and
r^{1}_{q}
in
\phi_{Q}

and between
r^{0}_{q}
and
r^{0}_{j}
in
\phi^{\prime}_{Q}
.
Thus,
P
is on the right side of
\phi_{Q}(r_{p})
and on the left side of
\phi^{\prime}_{Q}(r_{p})
, which can not be the case.
The second case is proven analogously.

Next, suppose the case

\phi_{Q}\|\{r_{q},r_{p},r_{j}\}^{*}\equiv r_{p}^{0}r_{j}^{1}r^{0}_{q}r_{p}^{1}r_{j}^{0}r_{q}^{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\phi^{\prime}_{Q}\|\{r_{q},r_{p},r_{j}\}^{*}\equiv r_{p}^{0}r_{j}^{0}r_{q}^{0}r_{p}^{1}r_{j}^{1}r_{q}^{1},

see Figure
9.1
to the right.
First, note that
\mathsf{inside}(M_{q})=\mathsf{inside}(M_{p})=\emptyset

as otherwise a P-node
P
from
\mathsf{inside}(M_{q})\cup\mathsf{inside}(M_{p})

would be on different sides of
\phi_{Q}(r_{j})
and
\phi^{\prime}_{Q}(r_{j})
.
Now, we show that for every node
P\in N_{\mathbb{T}}(Q)
,

P\in\mathsf{left}(r_{p})\iff P\in\mathsf{right}(r_{q}),

which shows
r_{p}Kr_{q}
and contradicts the fact that
r_{p}
and
r_{q}
are from different sets in
K(Q)
.
If
P\in\mathsf{left}(r_{p})\cap\mathsf{left}(r_{q})
, then

P
is on the right side of
\phi_{Q}(r_{j})
and on the left side of
\phi^{\prime}_{Q}(r_{j})
,
which can not be the case.
The second case is proven analogously.

The other cases corresponding to other placements of the chords
\phi_{Q}(r_{j})
and
\phi^{\prime}_{Q}(r_{j})

in the circular word
r_{p}^{0}r_{q}^{0}r_{p}^{1}r_{q}^{1}
are proven similarly.
This completes the proof of (
1
).

Let
\phi_{Q}
be an extended conformal model of
(Q,{\sim})
.
Statement (
2
) obviously holds
when
K=M_{i}
and
\mathsf{inside}(M_{i})\neq\emptyset
.
Suppose
K\subseteq\bigcup\{M_{i}:i\in[n]\text{ and }\mathsf{inside}(M_{i})=\emptyset\}
.
Let
P
be any P-node in
N_{\mathbb{T}}(Q)
; such a node exists as
G_{ov}
is disconnected.
Suppose without loss of generality that
K
is the union of
M_{1},\ldots,M_{m}
,
enumerated such that

\phi_{Q}\|(\{P\}\cup(M_{1}\cup\ldots\cup M_{m})^{*})\equiv P\lambda^{0}_{1}\ldots\lambda^{0}_{m}\lambda^{1}_{1}\ldots\lambda^{1}_{m},

where
(\lambda^{0}_{i},\lambda^{1}_{i})
is an oriented permutation model of
(M_{i},{\sim})
for
i\in[m]
–
see Figure
9.2
for an illustration.
In particular, the words
\lambda^{0}_{i}
and
\lambda^{1}_{i}
are contiguous in
\phi_{Q}
as
\mathsf{inside}(M_{i})=\emptyset
.
So, if
m=1
, then statement (
2
) holds.
Suppose
m\geqslant 2
.
Denote by
l^{0}
and
l^{1}
the first and the last letter in
\lambda^{0}_{1}
,
by
l^{2}
and
l^{3}
the first and the last letter in
\lambda^{0}_{m}
,
by
r^{0}
and
r^{1}
the first and the last letter in
\lambda^{1}_{1}
,
and by
r^{2}
and
r^{3}
the first and the last letter in
\lambda^{1}_{m}
–
see Figure
9.2
for an illustration.
Note that there is no letter from
N_{\mathbb{T}}(Q)
between
l^{1}
and
l^{2}
or
between
r^{1}
and
r^{2}
as otherwise
M_{1}
and
M_{m}
would not be in
K
-relation.
Suppose for a contrary that there is
v\in Q\smallsetminus K
such that
\phi_{Q}(v)
has
one end between
l^{1}
and
l^{2}
and the second end between
r^{1}
and
r^{2}
.
Now, our previous observation asserts that we have
vKM_{1}
and
vKM_{m}
, which is a contradiction as

v\notin K
.
Hence, the words
\lambda^{0}_{1}\ldots\lambda^{0}_{m}
and
\lambda^{1}_{1}\ldots\lambda^{1}_{m}
are contiguous in
\phi_{Q}
.
This completes the proof of statement (
2
).
∎

9.3.
Refinement procedure

Finally, we describe the refinement procedure that produces the CA-modules
\mathcal{S}(K)
for every
K\in K(Q)

and we show that
\mathcal{S}(K)
satisfies Properties
(R1):
–
(R3):
.

Suppose
K
is a member of
K(Q)
for some prime/serial component
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
.
For every strong module
L
in
\mathcal{M}(K,{\sim})
and every extended conformal model
\phi_{Q}
of
(Q,{\sim})
we denote by:

•

L^{0}
and
L^{1}
the sets
K^{0}\cap L^{*}
and
K^{1}\cap L^{*}
, respectively,

•

\tau(\phi_{Q},L^{j})
the shortest contiguous subword of
\phi_{Q}
containing
all the letters from
L^{j}
and no letter from
L^{1-j}
,

•

\mathsf{inside}(L)
the set of all P-nodes from
N_{\mathbb{T}}(Q)
that appear either in
\tau(\phi_{Q},L^{0})
or in

\tau(\phi_{Q},L^{1})
.

Since
|K(Q)|\geqslant 2
, the words
\tau(\phi_{Q},K^{j})
are properly defined and satisfy the statements of Claim
6.10
also for the case when
Q
is serial.
In particular, it shows that the set
\mathsf{inside}(L)
is properly defined.

To obtain a partition of
K
into CA-modules in
\mathcal{S}(K)
we perform the
refinement procedure
on
K
.
The procedure maintains a partition
\mathcal{S}(K)
of the set
K
into modules of
(K,{\sim})
.
The procedure marks each member of
\mathcal{S}(K)
either as
active
or
inactive
.
Initially, we set
\mathcal{S}(K)=\{K\}
, we mark
K
as active if
\mathsf{inside}(K)\neq\emptyset
, and as inactive if
\mathsf{inside}(K)=\emptyset
.
The procedure maintains the following invariants:

(I1)

Every active set
L\in S(K)
is a strong module in
\mathcal{M}(K,{\sim})
such that
\mathsf{inside}(L)\neq\emptyset
.

(I2)

For every extended conformal model
\phi_{Q}
of
(Q,{\sim})
, every
L,T\in\mathcal{S}(K)
, and every
j\in\{0,1\}
the letters from
L^{j}
do not overlap with the letters from
T^{j}
in the word
\tau(\phi,K^{j})
.
Moreover, if
\phi^{\prime}_{Q}
and
\phi_{Q}
are two extended conformal models of
(Q,{\sim})
such that
\phi_{Q}\|Q^{*}
and
\phi^{\prime}_{Q}\|Q^{*}
are admissible for
\gamma^{t}(Q)
for some
t\in\{0,1\}
, then the letters from
L^{j}
occur before the letters from
T^{j}
in
\tau(\phi_{Q},K^{j})
if and only if the letters from
L^{j}
occur before the letters from
T^{j}
in
\tau(\phi^{\prime}_{Q},K^{j})
.

The procedure is performed in steps as long as they are active modules in the set
\mathcal{S}(K)
.
In a single step, an active module
L
from
\mathcal{S}(K)
is partitioned into some active/inactive subsets of
L
.
When the procedure is over, all modules in
\mathcal{S}(S)
are inactive; we show that they form the set of all CA-modules of
K
.
Moreover, given
(I1)
and
(I2)
we show easily that the set
\mathcal{S}(K)
satisfies
Properties
(R1):
–
(R3):
.

Let
\phi_{Q}
and
\phi^{Q}
be two extended conformal models of
(Q,{\sim})
such that

\phi_{Q}\|Q^{*}
and
\phi^{\prime}_{Q}\|Q^{*}
are admissible for
\gamma^{t}(Q)
for some
t\in\{0,1\}
.
Let
\mathbb{K}=(K^{0},K^{1},{<_{K}})
be the metachord of
K
.
Assume that the set
K
induces in
\phi_{Q}\|Q^{*}
and
\phi^{\prime}_{Q}\|Q^{*}

consistent permutation models (they are admissible to
\mathbb{K}
) that correspond to the transitive orientations
{\prec_{\phi}}
and
{\prec_{\phi^{\prime}}}
of
(K,{\sim})
, respectively.

Suppose
L
is an active member of
\mathcal{S}(K)
such that
L
is prime in
\mathcal{M}(K,{\sim})
.
Suppose
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}
are the children of
L
in
\mathcal{M}(K,{\sim})
enumerated such that the letters from
L^{0}_{i}
occur before the letters from
L^{0}_{j}
in
\tau(\phi_{Q},K^{0})
for
i<j
.
Then we delete
L
from
\mathcal{S}(K)
, add
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}
to
\mathcal{S}(K)
, we mark
L_{i}
such that
\mathsf{inside}(L_{i})\neq\emptyset
as active and as inactive otherwise
– see Figure
9.3
for an illustration.
To show that Invariant
(I2)
is kept it suffices to prove that the transitive orientations
{\prec_{\phi}}
and
{\prec_{\phi^{\prime}}}
of

(K,{\sim})
restricted to the edges of
(L,{\sim}_{L})
are equal
(recall that
(L,{\sim_{L}})
contains all the edges from
(L,{\sim})
that have both endpoints in different children of
L
).
Since
L
is active, there is a letter
P
from
N_{\mathbb{T}}(Q)
such that
P\in\tau(\phi_{Q},L^{j})
for some
j\in\{0,1\}
.
Suppose
P\in\tau(\phi_{Q},L^{0})
.
Since
(L,{\sim})
is prime, there are

u\in L_{i}
and
v\in L_{j}

for some
L_{i}\sim L_{j}

such that
u^{\prime}Pv^{\prime}
is a subword of
\tau(\phi_{Q},L^{0})
for some
u^{\prime}\in\{u^{0},u^{1}\}
and
v^{\prime}\in\{v^{0},v^{1}\}
. This means
L_{i}\prec_{\phi}L_{j}
.
By Claim
6.10
,
u^{\prime}Pv^{\prime}
is also a subword of
\tau(\phi^{\prime}_{Q},L^{0})
,
and hence we have
L_{i}\prec_{\phi^{\prime}}L_{j}
.
Since
(L,{\sim_{L}})
has two transitive orientation, one being the reverse of the other, we deduce that

{\prec_{\phi}}
and
{\prec_{\phi^{\prime}}}
restricted to
(L,{\sim_{L}})
are equal.

\tau(\phi^{R}_{Q},L^{1})

Figure 9.3.

L
is prime.
We replace
L
in
\mathcal{S}(K)
by
L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},L_{4}
;
L_{1},L_{2}
are active,

Suppose
L
is an active member in
\mathcal{S}(K)
such that

is serial.
Suppose

are the children of

, enumerated such that

the letters from

appear before the letters from
R^{0}_{j}
in
\tau(\phi_{Q},K^{0})
–
see Figure

for an illustration.
Since
L
is serial,
for every
i<j
the letters from

appear before the letters from
R^{1}_{j}
in
\tau(\phi_{Q},K^{1})
.
Next, we define a partition
\mathcal{S}(L)
of the set
L
such that:

•

the set
R_{i}
is the member of

if
\mathsf{inside}(R_{i})\neq\emptyset
,

•

the set
R_{i}\cup R_{i+1}\cup\ldots\cup R_{j}
is the member of
\mathcal{S}(L)
if
[i,j]
is a maximal interval in
[t]
such that
\mathsf{inside}(R_{i}\cup R_{i+1}\cup\ldots\cup R_{j})=\emptyset
.

Suppose
L_{1},\ldots,L_{s}
are the members of
\mathcal{S}(L)
enumerated such that for every
i<j
the letters from
L^{0}_{i}
occur
before the letters from

in the word

.

.

To show that Invariant

.

.

.

from

as otherwise

and

.
It means that there are

,

,

.

6.10
.(

),

.

.
Since

L_{i}
and

, we have

.

–

.

•

.

.

.

10.
Linear-time algorithm constructing the PQSM-tree of a circular-arc graph.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a circular-arc graph with no twins and no universal vertices and let
G_{ov}
be the overlap graph.
In this section we present a linear-time algorithm that
constructs the PQSM-tree
\mathbb{T}^{*}
representing the conformal models of
G_{ov}
.

First, we compute the overlap graph
G_{ov}=(V,{\sim})
of
G
,
a conformal model
\phi
of
G_{ov}
and its reflection
\phi^{R}
.
Since the linear-time algorithm recognizing circular-arc graphs by McConnell
[
21
]

provides a normalized circular-arc model in the case of yes-instance,
we can use it to construct a normalized model
\psi
of
G
.
Then, we compute the overlap graph
(V,{\sim})
of
G
: for every
u,v\in V
we set
u\sim v
if and only if
uv\in E
and
\psi(u)
and
\psi(v)
overlap.
Eventually, we compute the conformal model
\phi
of
G_{ov}
corresponding to
\psi
and its reflection
\phi^{R}
.
Next, we use a linear-time algorithm of McConnell and Spinrad
[
22
]
to compute the modular decomposition tree
\mathcal{M}(G_{ov})
of
G_{ov}
.
Clearly, since
{\sim}\subseteq E
, all these steps can be done in linear-time in the size of
G
.

Now we show how we construct PQSM-tree
\mathbb{T}^{*}
.
Given
G_{ov}
and
\phi
, we first compute the set
\mathcal{S}
of CA-modules of
G
.
We leave it to the reader to verify that the set
\mathcal{S}
can be computed using Property
(R):
formulated in Subsection
5.2
.
We proceed the tree
\mathcal{M}(G_{ov})
in the bottom-up order and for every strong module
M
different than
V
we
compute the set
\mathcal{S}(M)
, as follows.
If
M
induces a contiguous permutation model
(\tau^{\prime}_{M},\tau^{\prime\prime}_{M})
in
\phi
,
we set
\mathcal{S}(M)=\emptyset
and we replace
\tau^{\prime}_{M}
and
\tau^{\prime\prime}_{M}
in
\phi

by the letters
M^{\prime}
and
M^{\prime\prime}
.
Otherwise, given that
M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}
are the children of
M
:

•

if
M
is serial/parallel, then

\mathcal{S}(M)
contains all the sets in
\mathcal{S}(M_{i})
for all
M_{i}
such that

\mathcal{S}(M_{i})\neq\emptyset
and all maximal modules
S\subseteq M
such that (i)
S
is the union of some children of
M
and (ii)
S
induces a contiguous permutation model in
\phi
,

•

if
M
is prime, then

\mathcal{S}(M)
contains all the sets in
\mathcal{S}(M_{i})
for
M_{i}
such that

\mathcal{S}(M_{i})\neq\emptyset
and all the sets
M_{i}
for which
\mathcal{S}(M_{i})=\emptyset
.

Finally, if
M
is a child of
V
and
\mathcal{S}(M)=\emptyset
, we set
\mathcal{S}(M)=\{M\}
.
We leave it to the reader to check that the set
\mathcal{S}(M)
comprises all CA-modules contained in
M
.
Since we can process each module in
\mathcal{M}(G_{ov})
in time linear in the number of its children,
we can compute the set
\mathcal{S}
in linear time in the size of
\mathcal{M}(G_{ov})
, and hence in linear time in the size of
G_{ov}
.
Since the set
\mathcal{S}
forms a partition of
V
into modules in
G_{ov}
,
we can compute the modular decomposition trees
\mathcal{M}(S,{\sim})
for all sets
S\in\mathcal{S}
in time linear in the size of
G_{ov}
.
Further, for all M-nodes
M
in
\mathcal{M}(S,{\sim})
we can read the representations of the sets

\Pi(M)
from the model
\phi
– see Subsection
5.4
.
All these steps can be done in linear time in the size of
G_{ov}
.

Finally, we show how to compute the sets
\Pi(N)
for every inner node
N
in the PQS-tree
\mathbb{T}
.
We consider only the case when
V
is parallel as the other cases are trivial.
Recall that the components of
G_{ov}
, whose set was denoted by
\mathcal{Q}
,
correspond to the Q-nodes of
\mathbb{T}
.
Given
\phi
, we first compute the extended conformal model
\phi[Q]
of
(Q,{\sim})
induced by
Q
.
For this purpose, for every component
Q
we compute a circular word
\phi^{\prime}_{Q}
,
where
\phi^{\prime}_{Q}
is obtained from
\phi\|Q^{*}
by inserting a letter
P^{\prime}

between every two letters
q^{\prime},q^{\prime\prime}\in Q^{*}
such that
q^{\prime}q^{\prime\prime}
is a contiguous subword in
\phi\|Q^{*}
but not in
\phi
.
We assume all the inserted letters
P^{\prime}
are pairwise different
and all belong to the set
\mathcal{P^{\prime}}
.
Clearly, we can compute
\phi^{\prime}_{Q}
from
\phi\|Q^{*}
in time linear in
|Q|
, and
hence, we can compute
\phi^{\prime}_{Q}
for all
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
in time linear in
|V|
.
Finally, given
\phi^{\prime}_{Q}
for all
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
,
in linear time we can compute all P-nodes of
\mathbb{T}
and the extended conformal models
\phi[Q]
for
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
.
For this purpose we use the following observation - see Figure
10.1
for an illustration.

Observation 10.1
.

Suppose
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}=\{Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{k}\}
is a subset of
\mathcal{Q}
.
Then,
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
is a P-node
if and only if there are
P^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,P^{\prime}_{k}
in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and

q^{\prime}_{j},q^{\prime\prime}_{j}
in
Q^{*}_{j}
for
j\in[k]
such that:

(1)

q_{j}^{\prime}P^{\prime}_{j}q_{j}^{\prime\prime}
is a contiguous subword of
\phi^{\prime}_{Q_{j}}
for every
j\in[k]
,

(2)

q_{j}^{\prime}q_{j+1}^{\prime\prime}
is a contiguous subword of
\phi
for every
j\in[k]
(cyclically).

Proof.

The necessity is obvious.
We prove the sufficiency.
Since
q_{j}^{\prime}q_{j+1}^{\prime\prime}
is a contiguous subword of
\phi
,
Q_{j}
and
Q_{j+1}
are neighbouring for all
j\in[k]
(cyclically).
Hence,
\{Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{k}\}
are pairwise neighbouring.
Also,
\{Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{k}\}
is a maximal set of pairwise neighbouring components;
otherwise,
q_{j}^{\prime}q_{j+1}^{\prime\prime}
for some
j\in[k]
(cyclically) would not be consecutive in
\phi
.
∎

q^{\prime\prime}_{1}
q^{\prime}_{1}
q^{\prime\prime}_{2}
q^{\prime}_{2}
q^{\prime\prime}_{3}
q^{\prime}_{3}
q^{\prime\prime}_{4}
q^{\prime}_{4}
Q_{1}
P^{\prime}_{1}
Q_{2}
P^{\prime}_{2}
Q_{3}
P^{\prime}_{3}
Q_{4}
P^{\prime}_{4}

Figure 10.1.
The letters
P^{\prime}_{1},P^{\prime}_{2},P^{\prime}_{3},P^{\prime}_{4}
are merged to a single P-node
P=\{Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3},Q_{4}\}
.

Given the set of P-nodes, the extended conformal models
\phi[Q]
, and the CA-modules of
G
,
we can compute easily
\mathbb{T}
and the sets
\Pi(\cdot)
for all inner nodes of
\mathbb{T}
.

11.
Linear-time canonization of a circular-arc graph

Let
G^{\prime}
be a circular-arc graph.
Let
U(G^{\prime})
denote the set of universal vertices of
G^{\prime}
and let

u=|U(G^{\prime})|
.
For every vertex
v\in V(G^{\prime})\smallsetminus U(G^{\prime})
let
T_{G^{\prime}}(v)
be the set of twins of
v
in
G^{\prime}
, that is,

T_{G^{\prime}}(v)=\{w\in V(G^{\prime}):N_{G^{\prime}}[v]=N_{G^{\prime}}[w]\}.

Clearly,
\{T_{G^{\prime}}(v):v\in V(G^{\prime})\smallsetminus U(G^{\prime})\}
forms a partition of
V(G)\smallsetminus U(G^{\prime})
.
Let
V
be the set containing a vertex from every set
\{T_{G^{\prime}}(v):v\in V(G^{\prime})\smallsetminus U(G^{\prime})\}
,
let
G
be a subgraph of
G^{\prime}
induced by
V
, and let
m(v)
for every
v\in V
be the size of the set
T_{G^{\prime}}(v)
.
Clearly,
G
is a circular-arc graph with no universal vertices and no twins.
The triple
(G,m,u)
is called the
representation
of
G^{\prime}
.

Claim 11.1
.

Let
G^{\prime}
be a circular-arc graph.
The representation
(G,m,u)
of
G^{\prime}
can be computed in linear time in the size of
G^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{M}(G^{\prime})
be the modular decomposition of
G^{\prime}
.
The following observations hold for every
v\in V(G^{\prime})
and every
T\subseteq V(G^{\prime})
such that
|T|\geqslant 2
:

•

v\in U(G^{\prime})
if and only if
V(G^{\prime})
is serial in
\mathcal{M}(G^{\prime})
and
\{v\}
is the child of
V(G^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{M}(G^{\prime})
.

•

T
is a set of twins in
G^{\prime}
if and only if
T\subseteq T^{\prime}
for some serial module
T^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{M}(G^{\prime})

and
T=\{u\in V(G^{\prime}):\{u\}\text{ is a child of $T^{\prime}$ in $\mathcal{M}(G^{\prime})$}\}
.

Since the modular decomposition tree
\mathcal{M}(G^{\prime})
can be computed in linear time in the size of
G^{\prime}
, in the same time we can compute the representation
(G,m,u)
of
G^{\prime}
.
∎

Suppose
G^{\prime}
and
H^{\prime}
are circular arc graphs represented by
(G,m_{G},u_{G})
and
(H,m_{H},u_{H})
.
We say that
(G,m_{G},u_{G})
and
(H,m_{H},u_{H})
are
isomorphic
if

u_{G}=u_{H}
and there is an isomorphism
\alpha
from
G
to
H
that satisfies
m_{G}(v)=m_{H}(\alpha(v))
for every
v\in V(G)
.
Clearly, all representations of
G^{\prime}
(corresponding to different choices of the vertices in
V
) are isomorphic.

Claim 11.2
.

G^{\prime}
and
H^{\prime}
are isomorphic if and only if
(G,m_{G},u_{G})
and
(H,m_{H},u_{H})
are isomorphic.

Our goal is to present a linear time algorithm that computes a string representation
\mathsf{canon}(G^{\prime})
of a circular-arc graph
G^{\prime}
.
For this purpose we compute the representation
(G,m_{G},u_{G})
of
G^{\prime}
, the overlap
graph
G_{ov}
of
G
, and the PQSM-tree
\mathbb{T}^{*}
representing the conformal models of
G_{ov}
.
For the reason that became clear later, for every node
L
in
\mathcal{M}(S,{\sim})
for
S\in\mathcal{S}
we define two metachords
\mathbb{L}^{0}
and
\mathbb{L}^{1}
:
\mathbb{L}^{0}=(L^{0},L^{1},{<^{0}_{L}})
and
\mathbb{L}^{1}=(L^{1},L^{0},{<^{1}_{L}})
, where
{<^{0}_{L}}={<_{L}}
and
<^{1}_{L}
is the reverse of
{<_{L}}
.
In particular, for every
S\in\mathcal{S}
model
\tau=(\tau^{0},\tau^{1})
is admissible for
\mathbb{S}^{0}
if and only if model
(\tau^{1},\tau^{0})

is admissible for
\mathbb{S}^{1}
.
We set

\Pi(\mathbb{L}^{0})=\Pi(L)\quad\text{and}\quad\Pi(\mathbb{L}^{1})=\{(\pi^{1},\pi^{0}):(\pi^{0},\pi^{1})\in\Pi(L)\}.

Also, we let

\begin{array}[]{ccl}\mathcal{L}^{0}&=&\{\mathbb{L}^{0}:L\text{ is a node in }\mathcal{M}(S,{\sim})\text{ for some }S\in\mathcal{S}\},\\
\mathcal{L}^{1}&=&\{\mathbb{L}^{1}:L\text{ is a node in }\mathcal{M}(S,{\sim})\text{ for some }S\in\mathcal{S}\},\\
\mathcal{L}&=&\{L:L\text{ is a node in }\mathcal{M}(S,{\sim})\text{ for some }S\in\mathcal{S}\}.\end{array}

We have shown in Section
10
that we can compute these components in time linear in the size of
G
.

We partition the members of
\mathcal{L}
into
levels
:
for every
S\in\mathcal{S}
and every
L\in\mathcal{M}(S,{\sim})
we have

\mathsf{level}(L)=l
if the distance between
L
and
S
in
\mathcal{M}(S,{\sim})

equals to
l
.
In particular,
\mathsf{level}(S)=0
for every
S\in\mathcal{S}
.
We assume
\mathsf{level}(\mathbb{L}^{0})=\mathsf{level}(\mathbb{L}^{1})=\mathsf{level}(L)
for every
L\in\mathcal{L}
.
By
\mathcal{L}_{l}
,
(\mathcal{L}^{0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{1})_{l}

we denote the restrictions of the sets
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}^{0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{1}
, respectively, to the elements from level
l
.
We assume
\mathcal{L}_{l,l+1}=\mathcal{L}_{l}\cup\mathcal{L}_{l+1}
and similarly for the set
(\mathcal{L}^{0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{1})_{l,l+1}
.

Similarly, we partition the nodes of the PQS-tree
\mathbb{T}
into levels with respect to the
root
of
\mathbb{T}
.
To define the root of
\mathbb{T}
we need some definitions.
A
center
of a tree is a vertex with the minimum maximum distance to a leaf.
It is commonly known that any tree has either one center (and then the tree is called
centered
) or has two centers joined with an edge (and then the tree is called
bicentered
).
Next, we root the tree
\mathbb{T}
such that:

•

if
\mathbb{T}
is centered, we root
\mathbb{T}
in the center of
\mathbb{T}
,

•

if
\mathbb{T}
is bicentered, we add a special node
R
on the edge joining two centers of
\mathbb{T}
(one is a Q-node and one is a P-node) and we root
\mathbb{T}
in the node
R
.

Let
N(\mathbb{T})
denote the set of the nodes of
\mathbb{T}
.
For
N\in N(\mathbb{T})
by
\mathsf{level}(N)
we denote the distance of
N
to the root of

\mathbb{T}
.
We let
N(\mathbb{T})_{l}
and
N(\mathbb{T})_{l,l+1}
to contain the nodes of
\mathbb{T}

whose distance to the root is in the sets
\{l\}
and
\{l,l+1\}
, respectively.
Eventually, for every inner node
N
in
\mathbb{T}
, by
V_{\mathbb{T}}(N)

we denote the vertices of
V
from the components of
G_{ov}

corresponding to Q-nodes contained in the subtree of
\mathbb{T}
rooted in
N

(the set
V_{\mathbb{T}}(N)
contains also the vertices from
N
if
N
is a Q-node).

Our goal is to compute a string representation
\mathsf{canon}(G,m_{G},u_{G})
of
(G,m_{G},u_{G})
such that for every other circular arc graph
H^{\prime}
represented by
(H,m_{H},u_{H})
:

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathsf{canon}(G,m_{G},u_{G})=\mathsf{canon}(H,m_{H},u_{H})\quad\iff\\
\text{$(G,m_{G},u_{G})$ and $(H,m_{H},u_{H})$ are isomorphic.}\end{array}

Finally, we set
\mathsf{canon}(G^{\prime})=\mathsf{canon}(G,m_{G},u_{G})
and we note that, by Claim
11.2
,
\mathsf{canon}(G^{\prime})=\mathsf{canon}(H^{\prime})
if and only if
G^{\prime}
and
H^{\prime}
are isomorphic.

To compute
\mathsf{canon}(G,m_{G},u_{G})
we first calculate the number
\mathsf{num}(X)
for every
object
X
in
\mathcal{L}^{0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{1}\cup N(\mathbb{T})
.
We compute
\mathsf{num}(\cdot)
in two steps, first for the objects in
\mathcal{L}^{0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{1}
, and then for the nodes in
\mathbb{T}
.
In each step, we process the objects in the decreasing order of their levels;
that is, the objects at level
l+1
are processed
before the objects at level
l
.
Two objects
X,Y
on the same level will satisfy
\mathsf{num}(X)=\mathsf{num}(Y)
if and only if
X
and
Y
are “locally isomorphic”, where the meaning of a “ local isomorphism” depends on the type of the objects
(informally, two objects
X,Y
are locally isomorphic if the parts of the graph
G
induced by the vertices from the subtrees rooted at
X
and at
Y
are isomorphic).
Given computed the numbers
\mathsf{num}(\cdot)
for the objects from level
l+1
, we
process the objects from level
l
.
First, we compute a tuple
\mathsf{canon}(X)
for every object
X
from level
l
.
The tuple
\mathsf{canon}(X)
encodes the object
X
(in particular, it uses the numbers
\mathsf{num}
encoding the local isomorphism type of the children of
X
from level
l+1
)
so as for two objects
X
and
Y
at level
l
we have
\mathsf{canon}(X)=\mathsf{canon}(Y)
if and only if
X
and
Y
are locally isomorphic.
Roughly speaking,
\mathsf{canon}(X)
is the lexicographically smallest tuple among
appropriately defined linear representations of the members of the set
\Pi(X)
.
We sort all the tuples
\mathsf{canon}(X)
for the objects
X
from level
l

and we identify those that share
\mathsf{canon}(\cdot)
(those that are locally isomorphic).
All the tuples
\mathsf{canon}(\cdot)
are stored in the table
\mathsf{canon}
,
indexed with the consecutive natural numbers starting from
Num=0
.
The variable
Num
always indicates the first free index in the
\mathsf{canon}
table.
The tuple
\mathsf{canon}(X)
for the object
X
is stored at the position
\mathsf{num}(X)
, that is,

\mathsf{canon}(X)=\mathsf{canon}[\mathsf{num}(X)]\quad\text{holds for every object }X\in\mathcal{L}^{0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{1}\cup N(\mathbb{T}).

Summing up, the group of locally isomorphic objects is represented by one entry in the table
\mathsf{canon}
,
stored at the position
\mathsf{num}
which is common for the objects of this group
(that is,
\mathsf{num}=\mathsf{num}(X)
for every object
X
in this group).
The tuples
\mathsf{canon}
for objects from the same level are stored in a contiguous block of the table
\mathsf{canon}
.
Eventually,
\mathsf{canon}(G,m_{G},u_{G})
is defined as a linearisation of the table
\mathsf{canon}
.

We start by showing how the algorithm computes the values
\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})
and
\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})
for the metachords
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
in the set
(\mathcal{L}^{0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{1})_{l}
.
We assume that
\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})
and
\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})
are computed for all
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\in(\mathcal{L}^{0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{1})_{l+1}
.
We assume also that
\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})=\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{R}^{\prime})
holds for every two metachords
\mathbb{L}^{\prime},\mathbb{R}^{\prime}\in(\mathcal{L}^{0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{1})_{l+1}

if and only if
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}
are locally isomorphic according to the following definition.

Definition 11.3
.

Suppose
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}=(L^{\prime},L^{\prime\prime},{<^{\prime}_{L}})
and
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}=(R^{\prime},R^{\prime\prime},{<^{\prime}_{R}})
are two metachords such that
\mathsf{level}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})=\mathsf{level}(\mathbb{R}^{\prime})
, where

\mathbb{L}^{\prime}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}(L^{0},L^{1},{<^{0}_{L}})&\text{ if }&\mathbb{L^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{L}^{0}\\
(L^{1},L^{0},{<^{1}_{L}})&\text{ if }&\mathbb{L^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{L}^{1}\\
\end{array}\right.\text{ and }\quad\mathbb{R}^{\prime}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}(R^{0},R^{1},{<^{0}_{R}})&\text{ if }&\mathbb{R}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}^{0}\\
(R^{1},R^{0},{<^{1}_{R}})&\text{ if }&\mathbb{R}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}^{1}\\
\end{array}\right.

Let
\alpha
be a bijection from
L
to
R
.
We say that
\alpha
is a
local isomorphism
from
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
to
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}
if for every
u,v\in L
:

(1)

u<^{\prime}_{L}v\iff\alpha(u)<^{\prime}_{R}\alpha(v)
,

and for every
u\in L
:

(2)

u
is oriented from
L^{\prime}
to
L^{\prime\prime}

\iff

\alpha(u)
is
oriented from
R^{\prime}
to
R^{\prime\prime}
,

(3)

m_{G}(u)=m_{G}(\alpha(u))
.

We say
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}
are
locally isomorphic
if there is a local isomorphism from
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
to
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}
.

Let
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}=(L^{\prime},L^{\prime\prime},{<^{\prime}_{L}})
is a metachord from
(\mathcal{L}^{0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{1})_{l}
and let
S\in\mathcal{S}
be such that
L\subseteq S
.
The tuple
\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})
is defined as follows:

•

If
L=\{u\}
is a leaf in
\mathcal{M}(S,{\sim})
, then

\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})=\big([u^{0}\in L^{\prime}],m_{G}(u)+Num\big),

where
[u^{0}\in L^{\prime}]=1
if
u^{0}\in L^{\prime}
and
[u^{0}\in L^{\prime}]=0
if
u^{0}\notin L^{\prime}
.

•

If
L
in a non-leaf in
\mathcal{M}(S,{\sim})
with children
L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}
,
then
\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})
is the lexicographically smallest tuple

\mathsf{canon}\big(\mathbb{L}^{\prime},\pi(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})\big)
over all members
\pi(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})
of
\Pi(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})
,
where for
\pi(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})\in\Pi(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})
of the form
\pi(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})=\big(L^{\prime}_{\delta^{\prime}(1)},\ldots,L^{\prime}_{\delta^{\prime}(k)}),(L^{\prime\prime}_{\delta^{\prime\prime}(1)},\ldots,L^{\prime\prime}_{\delta^{\prime\prime}(k)})\big)
for some permutations
\delta^{\prime},\delta^{\prime\prime}
of
[k]
we set

\begin{array}[]{lcc}\mathsf{canon}\big(\mathbb{L}^{\prime},\pi(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})\big)&=&\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\ \mathsf{num}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime}_{\delta^{\prime}(1)}),\ pos\big(L^{\prime\prime}_{\delta^{\prime}(1)},(L^{\prime\prime}_{\delta^{\prime\prime}(1)},\ldots,L^{\prime\prime}_{\delta^{\prime\prime}(k)})\big),\\
\vdots\\
\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime}_{\delta^{\prime}(k)}),\ pos\big(L^{\prime\prime}_{\delta^{\prime}(k)},(L^{\prime\prime}_{\delta^{\prime\prime}(1)},\ldots,L_{\delta^{\prime\prime}(k)})\big),\end{array}\right),\end{array}

where
pos\big(L^{\prime\prime}_{\delta^{\prime}(i)},(L^{\prime\prime}_{\delta^{\prime\prime}(1)},\ldots,L^{\prime\prime}_{\delta^{\prime\prime}(k)})\big)
is the position of
L^{\prime\prime}_{i}
in
(L^{\prime\prime}_{\delta^{\prime\prime}(1)},\ldots,L^{\prime\prime}_{\delta^{\prime\prime}(k)})
increased by the current value of
Num
.
See Figure
11.1
for an example.

Note that the type of the module
L
can be easily recovered from the entries
pos(\cdot)
.

L^{0}_{1}
L^{0}_{2}
L^{0}_{3}
L^{0}_{4}
L^{1}_{3}
L^{1}_{1}
L^{1}_{4}
L^{1}_{2}
v^{0}_{1}
v^{1}_{2}
v^{1}_{3}
v^{1}_{4}
v^{0}_{5}
v^{0}_{6}
v^{1}_{7}
v^{0}_{8}
v^{1}_{9}
v^{0}_{10}
v^{1}_{6}
v^{0}_{7}
v^{1}_{1}
v^{0}_{3}
v^{0}_{2}
v^{0}_{9}
v^{0}_{8}
v^{1}_{10}
v^{1}_{4}
v^{1}_{5}

Figure 11.1.

The pair
\big((L^{0}_{1},L^{0}_{2},L^{0}_{3},L^{0}_{4}),(L^{1}_{2},L^{1}_{4},L^{1}_{1},L^{1}_{3})\big)
is in
\Pi(\mathbb{L}^{0})
.
Assuming
Num=0
we have
canon\Big(\mathbb{L},\big((L^{0}_{1},L^{0}_{2},L^{0}_{3},L^{0}_{4}),(L^{1}_{2},L^{1}_{4},L^{1}_{1},L^{1}_{3})\big)\Big)=\big(num(\mathbb{L}^{0}_{1}),3,num(\mathbb{L}^{0}_{2}),1,num(\mathbb{L}^{0}_{3}),4,num(\mathbb{L}^{0}_{4}),2\big).

Claim 11.4
.

Let
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}
be two metachords from
(\mathcal{L}^{0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{1})_{l}
.
Then,
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}
are locally isomorphic if and only if
\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})=canon(\mathbb{R}^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Suppose
L
is a strong module in
\mathcal{M}(S_{L},{\sim})
and
R
is a strong module in
\mathcal{M}(S_{R},{\sim})
for some
S_{L},S_{R}\in\mathcal{S}
.

Assume that
\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})=\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{R}^{\prime})
.
Suppose
L
and
R
are leaves, say,
L=\{u\}
and
R=\{u^{\prime}\}
.
Since
\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})=\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{R}^{\prime})
, the mapping
\alpha:L\to R
such that
\alpha(u)=u^{\prime}
establishes a local isomorphism between
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}
.
Suppose
L
and
R
are non-leaves.
Let
L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}
be the children of
L
, enumerated such that

\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})=\mathsf{canon}\Big(\mathbb{L}^{\prime},\big((L^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,L^{\prime}_{k}),(L^{\prime\prime}_{\delta(1)},\ldots,L^{\prime\prime}_{\delta(k)})\big)\Big)

for some
\big((L^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,L^{\prime}_{k}),(L^{\prime\prime}_{\delta(1)},\ldots,L^{\prime\prime}_{\delta(k)})\big)\in\Pi(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})
.
Let
R_{1},\ldots,R_{k}
be the children of
R
, enumerated such that

\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{R}^{\prime})=\mathsf{canon}\Big(\mathbb{R}^{\prime},\big((R^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,R^{\prime}_{k}),(R^{\prime\prime}_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,R^{\prime\prime}_{\sigma(k)})\big)\Big)

for some
\big((R^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,R^{\prime}_{k}),(R^{\prime\prime}_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,R^{\prime\prime}_{\sigma(k)})\big)\in\Pi(\mathbb{R}^{\prime})
.
Since
\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})=\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{R}^{\prime})
, we have

\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime}_{i})=\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{R}^{\prime}_{i})
and
\delta=\sigma
.
Since
\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime}_{i})=\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{R}^{\prime}_{i})
,
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}_{i}
is locally isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}_{i}
for every
i\in[k]
.
Suppose that
\alpha_{i}
establishes a local isomorphism between
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}_{i}
and
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}_{i}
.
We claim that
\alpha:L\to R
, where
\alpha=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\alpha_{i}
, establishes a local isomorphism between

\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}
.
Clearly,
\alpha
satisfies conditions
11.3
.(
2
)–(
3
) as
\alpha_{i}
satisfies

11.3
.(
2
)–(
3
) for every
i\in[k]
.
Also,
\alpha
satisfies condition
11.3
.(
1
) as

\alpha_{i}
satisfies
11.3
.(
1
) and
\delta=\sigma
.

Suppose
\alpha
is a local isomorphism between
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}
.
If
L
and
R
are leaves, then

\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})=\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{R}^{\prime})
as
\alpha
satisfies conditions
11.3
.(
2
)–(
3
).
If
L
and
R
are non-leaves, then
L
and
R
have the same number of children in
\mathcal{M}(S_{L},{\sim})
and in
\mathcal{M}(S_{R},{\sim})
, respectively.
Suppose
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{L}^{\prime}_{k}
are the restrictions of
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
to the sets
L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}
and

\mathbb{R}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{R}^{\prime}_{k}
are the restrictions of
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}
to the sets
R_{1},\ldots,R_{k}
, where

L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}
and
R_{1},\ldots,R_{k}
are the children of
L
and
R
, respectively, enumerated such that
\alpha(L_{i})=R_{i}
for every
i\in[k]
.
Clearly,
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{L}^{\prime}_{k}
and
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{R}^{\prime}_{k}
are from level
l+1
.
Now, note that the mapping

\big((L^{\prime}_{\delta^{\prime}(1)},\ldots,L^{\prime}_{\delta^{\prime}(k)}),(L^{\prime\prime}_{\delta^{\prime\prime}(1)},\ldots,L^{\prime\prime}_{\delta^{\prime\prime}(k)})\big)\to\big((R^{\prime}_{\delta^{\prime}(1)},\ldots,R^{\prime}_{\delta^{\prime}(k)}),(R^{\prime\prime}_{\delta^{\prime\prime}(1)},\ldots,R^{\prime\prime}_{\delta^{\prime\prime}(k)})\big)

establishes a bijection between the members of
\Pi(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})
and the members of
\Pi(\mathbb{R}^{\prime})
.
Since
\alpha|L_{i}
establishes a local isomorphism between
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}_{i}
and
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}_{i}
, we must have
\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{L}_{i})=\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{R}_{i})
and
\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{L}_{i})=\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{R}_{i})
for every
i\in[k]
.
Thus, we have
\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})=\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{R}^{\prime})
.
∎

Next, we claim we can compute
\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})
and
\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})
for all
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\in(\mathcal{L}^{0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{1})_{l}

in total time linear in the size of the set
(\mathcal{L}^{0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{1})_{l,l+1}
.
Clearly, if
L
is prime or parallel, then
|\Pi(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})|\leqslant 2
, and
\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})
can be computed in linear time in the number of the children of
L
.
Suppose
L
is serial and suppose
L_{1},\ldots,L_{k}
are the children of
L
.
To compute
\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})
it suffices to sort the numbers in the tuple
(\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime}_{1}),\ldots,\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime}_{k}))
.
Note that the numbers
\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime}_{i})
are integers from the interval whose length is bounded by the size of
(\mathcal{L}^{0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{1})_{l+1}
.
Since the total number of the entries in the tuples
(\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime}_{1}),\ldots,\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime}_{k}))
for all
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\in(\mathcal{L}^{0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{1})_{l}

is bounded by the size of
(\mathcal{L}^{0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{1})_{l+1}
,
we can sort the entries in all such tuples in time linear in the size of
(\mathcal{L}^{0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{1})_{l+1}
, which follows from the following proposition.

Proposition 11.5
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a set of arbitrarily length tuples,
t
be the total number of the entries in
\mathcal{T}
, and
d
be the difference between the maximum and the minimum entry among all the tuples from
\mathcal{T}
.
We can sort the entries of every tuple from
\mathcal{T}
in time
\mathcal{O}(t+d)
.

Proof.

We use a modified
counting-sort
algorithm.
First, we find the minimum entry
Min
in all the tuples from
\mathcal{T}
and we subtract
Min
from every entry of every tuple in
\mathcal{T}
.
Now, every entry is in the interval
[0,d]
.
Next, for every
x\in[0,d]
we compute the set
p(x)
containing the pointers to the tuples
T
from
\mathcal{T}
which contain
x
.
We clear all the tuples in
\mathcal{T}
.
Then, we proceed the table
p
from
d
down to
0
,
and for every pointer to the tuple
T
in the set
p(x)

we insert the entry
(x+Min)
to the tuple
T
at the first position.
Clearly, the algorithm works in time
O(t+d)
and sorts the entries of all tuples in
\mathcal{T}
.
∎

Suppose that the tuples
\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})
are computed for all
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\in(\mathcal{L}^{0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{1})_{l}
.
Note that the total number of the entries in all those tuples, excluding those corresponding to the leaves
L
in
\mathcal{L}_{l}
(one entry in
\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{L}^{0})
and
\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{L}^{1})
encodes the number of twins of the vertex in
L
), is linearly bounded by the size of the set
(\mathcal{L}^{0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{1})_{l,l+1}

and every such entry is an integer from the interval whose length is linearly bounded by the size of
(\mathcal{L}^{0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{1})_{l,l+1}
.
The next proposition asserts we can sort all those tuples in time linear in the size of
(\mathcal{L}^{0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{1})_{l,l+1}
.

Proposition 11.6

(
[
1
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a set of arbitrarily length tuples,
t
be the total number of the entries in
\mathcal{T}
, and
d
be the difference between the maximum and the minimum entry among all the tuples from
\mathcal{T}
.
We can lexicographically sort the tuples from
\mathcal{T}
in time
\mathcal{O}(t+d)
.

Also, we can sort the tuples
\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})
for leaf metachords
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\in(\mathcal{L}^{0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{1})_{l}
in time linear in

\sum\{m(v):\{v\}\text{ is a leaf module from }(\mathcal{L})_{l}\}
.

Let
n=|V(G^{\prime})|
.
Since
|\mathcal{L}^{0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{1}|\leqslant 4n
, we deduce that we can perform the first step of the canonization procedure in time
\mathcal{O}(n)
.

We proceed to the second step of the canonization procedure.
Our goal is to compute
\mathsf{canon}(N)
and
\mathsf{num}(N)
for every inner node of the PQS-tree
\mathbb{T}
;
for leaves
S^{0}
and
S^{1}
(which are slots of
G
)
in
\mathbb{T}
we set
\mathsf{canon}(S^{j})=\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{S}^{j})
and

\mathsf{num}(S^{j})=\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{S}^{j})
for
j\in\{0,1\}
.
We assume that
\mathsf{num}(N_{1})=\mathsf{num}(N_{2})
for every two locally isomorphic nodes
N_{1},N_{2}
in
N(\mathbb{T})_{l+1}
,
where the local isomorphism between the inner nodes of
\mathbb{T}
is defined as follows.

Definition 11.7
.

Suppose
N_{1},N_{2}
are two inner nodes of the PQS-tree
\mathbb{T}
such that
\mathsf{level}(N_{1})=\mathsf{level}(N_{2})
.
Let
\alpha
be a bijection from
V_{\mathbb{T}}(N_{1})
to
V_{\mathbb{T}}(N_{2})
.
We say
\alpha
is a
local isomorphism
between
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
if for every
u,v\in V_{\mathbb{T}}(N_{1})
:

(1)

m_{G}(u)=m_{G}(\alpha(u))
,

(2)

u\in\mathsf{left}(v)\iff\alpha(u)\in\mathsf{left}(\alpha(v))
,

(3)

u\in\mathsf{right}(v)\iff\alpha(u)\in\mathsf{right}(\alpha(v))
.

Moreover, if
N^{\prime}_{1}
and
N^{\prime}_{2}
are the parents of
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
in
\mathbb{T}
,
for every
v\in V_{\mathbb{T}}(N_{1})
:

(4)

N^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathsf{left}(v)\iff N^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathsf{left}(\alpha(v))
.

(5)

N^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathsf{right}(v)\iff N^{\prime}_{2}\in\mathsf{right}(\alpha(v))
.

Observe that if
\alpha
establishes a local isomorphism from
N_{1}
to
N_{2}
, then:

•

if
N_{1},N_{2}\in N(\mathbb{T})_{l}
for some
l\geqslant 1
,
then both
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
are either P-nodes or Q-nodes,

•

if
N_{1}=N_{2}
is the root of
\mathbb{T}
,
then
\alpha
is an isomorphism from
(G,m_{G},u_{G})
to
(G,m_{G},u_{G})
.

Suppose
\alpha
is a local isomorphism between
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
.
For convenience, we introduce the concept of the “image by
\alpha
” for words and subsets of
V^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(N_{1})\cup V_{\mathbb{T}}(N_{1})
.
Suppose
w_{1}
is a word consisting of some letters from
V^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(N_{1})\cup V_{\mathbb{T}}(N_{1})
.
The
image of
\tau
by
\alpha
, denoted by
\alpha(\tau)
, is a word
that arises from
\tau
by replacing every letter
u^{i}
of
\tau
from
V^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(N_{1})
by
\alpha(u)^{i}
and every letter
u
of
\tau
from
V_{\mathbb{T}}(N_{1})
by
\alpha(u)
.
We use an analogous notation for subsets of
V^{*}_{\mathbb{T}}(N_{1})\cup V_{\mathbb{T}}(N_{1})
.

Now, we define
\mathsf{canon}(\cdot)
for every node in
N(\mathbb{T})_{l}
.
Suppose
Q
is a Q-node in
N(\mathbb{T})_{l}
.
We transform every circular word
\pi(Q)
in
\Pi(Q)
into a tuple
\mathsf{canon}(Q,\pi(Q))
, as follows.
First, we let
\pi^{\prime}(Q)
to be a circular tuple that arises from the circular word
\pi(Q)
such that:

•

for every
S\in\mathcal{S}(Q)
and every
i\in\{0,1\}
we replace the slot
S^{i}
in
\pi(Q)
by two entries,
\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{S}^{i})
and
dist(S^{i},S^{1-i},\pi(Q))
,
where
dist(S^{i},S^{1-i},\pi(Q))
is the number of the letters between
S^{i}
and
S^{1-i}
in
\pi(Q)
increased by the current value of
Num
.

•

for every P-node
P
neighbouring
Q
from level
(l+1)
we
replace
P
in
\pi(Q)
by the entry
\mathsf{num}(P)
.

If
Q
has a parent
P
in
\mathbb{T}
, we set
\mathsf{canon}(Q,\pi(Q))
such that the circular word
P\cdot\mathsf{canon}(Q,\pi(Q))
equals to
\pi^{\prime}(Q)
(note that
\mathsf{canon}(Q,\pi(Q))
is a non-circular word).
Otherwise (
Q
is the root of
\mathbb{T}
),
we set
\mathsf{canon}(Q,\pi(Q))
as the lexicographically smallest word
that satisfies
\pi^{\prime}(Q)\equiv\mathsf{canon}(Q,\pi(Q))
(i.e.
\mathsf{canon}(Q,\pi(Q))

is the lexicographically smallest word which made circular gives
\pi^{\prime}(Q)
).
Eventually, we set
\mathsf{canon}(Q)
as the lexicographically smallest tuple in the set
\{\mathsf{canon}(Q,\pi(Q)):\pi(Q)\in\Pi(Q)\}
.

We claim that the tuple
\mathsf{canon}(Q)

can be computed in time linear in the size of the set
\mathcal{S}(Q)
.
If
Q
has a parent in the PQS-tree
\mathbb{T}
,

\Pi(Q)
has exactly two admissible orders, each of size at most
4|\mathcal{S}(Q)|
,
and hence
\mathsf{canon}(Q)
can be computed in linear time in
|\mathcal{S}(Q)|
.
Suppose
Q
is the root of
\mathbb{T}
.
Again, the set
\Pi(Q)
contains exactly two elements,
each of size at most
4|\mathcal{S}(Q)|
, for the cases where
V
is parallel/prime in
\mathcal{M}(G_{ov})
.
Moreover, each entry in the tuple
\pi^{\prime}(Q)
is contained in the interval whose length is linearly bounded in
|N(\mathbb{T})|
(and hence in time linear in the size of
G
).
The next proposition asserts we can compute
\mathsf{canon}(Q)
in linear time in
|N(\mathbb{T})|
.

Proposition 11.8

(
[
3
]
)
.

Suppose
\pi^{\prime}
is a circular word of size
t
whose letters are integers from the interval of length at most
d
.
Then, in time
\mathcal{O}(t+d)
we can compute the lexicographically smallest (simple, non-circular) word
\pi

such that
\pi\equiv\pi^{\prime}
.

Finally, assume that
Q
is serial in
\mathcal{M}(G_{ov})
.
That is, we have
Q=V
.
In this case we have

\mathsf{canon}(V)=\begin{array}[]{l}\Big(\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{S}^{\prime}_{1}),Num+|\mathcal{S}|-1,\ldots,\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{S}^{\prime}_{k}),Num+|\mathcal{S}|-1,\\
\ \ \mathsf{num}(\mathbb{S}^{\prime\prime}_{1}),Num+|\mathcal{S}|-1,\ldots,\mathsf{canon}(\mathbb{S}^{\prime\prime}_{k}),Num+|\mathcal{S}|-1\Big),\end{array}

where
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}_{i},\mathbb{S}^{\prime\prime}_{i}
are such that
\{\mathbb{S}^{\prime},\mathbb{S}^{\prime\prime}\}=\{\mathbb{S}^{0},\mathbb{S}^{1}\}
and
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}_{i}=min\{\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{S}^{0}_{i}),\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{S}^{1}_{i})\}
,
and the sets
S_{1},\ldots,S_{k}
in
\mathcal{S}
are enumerated such that we have
\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{S}^{\prime}_{i})\leqslant\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{S}^{\prime}_{j})
for every
i<j
.
Hence, to compute
\mathsf{canon}(V)
it suffices to sort the numbers from the set
\{\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{S}^{\prime}_{1}),\ldots,\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{S}^{\prime}_{k})\}
, which can be done in linear time in
|\mathcal{S}|
.

Claim 11.9
.

Suppose
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
are two Q-nodes such that
\mathsf{level}(Q_{1})=\mathsf{level}(Q_{2})
with parents
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
in
\mathbb{T}
.
Then,
Q_{1}
is locally isomorphic to
Q_{2}
if and only if
\mathsf{canon}(Q_{1})=\mathsf{canon}(Q_{2})
.

Proof.

Suppose
\alpha
is a local isomorphism between
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
.
Note that
\alpha
maps the vertices of
Q_{1}
into the vertices of
Q_{2}
.
Moreover,
\phi\|Q^{*}_{1}
is a conformal model of
(Q_{1},{\sim})
and the image
\alpha(\phi\|Q^{*}_{1})
of
\phi\|Q^{*}_{1}
by
\alpha
is a conformal model of
(Q_{2},{\sim})
.
Moreover, observe that:

•

For every
S\in\mathcal{S}(Q_{1})
, the image
\alpha(S)
of a CA-module
S\in\mathcal{S}(Q_{1})
is a CA-module in
\mathcal{S}(Q_{2})
and
the mapping
S\to\alpha(S)
for
S\in\mathcal{S}(Q_{1})
establishes a bijection between the sets in
\mathcal{S}(Q_{1})
and the sets in
\mathcal{S}(Q_{2})
.

•

For every
S\in\mathcal{S}(Q_{1})
the images
\alpha(S^{0}),\alpha(S^{1})
of the slots
S^{0},S^{1}
of
S
are the slots of
\alpha(S)
.
Moreover,
\alpha
restricted to
S
establishes a local isomorphism between the metachords
\mathbb{S}^{0},\mathbb{S}^{1}

and
\alpha(\mathbb{S}^{0}),\alpha(\mathbb{S}^{1})
, respectively.
Hence we have
\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{S}^{0})=\mathsf{num}(\alpha(\mathbb{S}^{0}))

and
\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{S}^{1})=\mathsf{num}(\alpha(\mathbb{S}^{1}))
.

•

For every
P\in N_{\mathbb{T}}(Q)\smallsetminus\{P_{1}\}
,
\alpha
maps the set
V_{\mathbb{T}}(P)
into the set
V_{\mathbb{T}}(P^{\prime})
for some
P^{\prime}\in N_{\mathbb{T}}(Q_{2})\smallsetminus\{P_{2}\}
and
\alpha
restricted to
V_{\mathbb{T}}(P)
establishes a local isomorphism between
P
and
P^{\prime}
. Hence we have
\mathsf{num}(P)=\mathsf{num}(P^{\prime})
.

Given the above observations we easily check that
\mathsf{canon}(Q_{1})=\mathsf{canon}(Q_{2})
.

Suppose
\mathsf{canon}(Q_{1})=\mathsf{canon}(Q_{2})
.
Let
\pi(Q_{1})\in\Pi(Q_{1})
and
\pi(Q_{2})\in\Pi(Q_{2})
be such that

\mathsf{canon}(Q_{1})=\mathsf{canon}(Q_{1},\pi(Q_{1}))\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{canon}(Q_{2})=\mathsf{canon}(Q_{2},\pi(Q_{2})).

Let
P,P^{\prime}
be nodes from
N_{\mathbb{T}}(Q_{1})\smallsetminus\{P_{1}\}
and
N_{\mathbb{T}}(Q_{2})\smallsetminus\{P_{2}\}
, respectively,
such that
\mathsf{num}(P)
and
\mathsf{num}(P^{\prime})
appear in
\mathsf{canon}(Q_{1},\pi(Q_{1}))
and
\mathsf{canon}(Q_{2},\pi(Q_{2}))
at the same position.
Since
\mathsf{canon}(Q_{1},\pi(Q_{1}))=\mathsf{canon}(Q_{2},\pi(Q_{2}))
,
\mathsf{num}(P)=\mathsf{num}(P^{\prime})
, and hence
P
is locally isomorphic to
P^{\prime}
.
Suppose
\alpha_{P}:V_{\mathbb{T}}(P)\to V_{\mathbb{T}}(P^{\prime})
establishes a local isomorphism between
P
and
P^{\prime}
.

Let
S
be a CA-module from
\mathcal{S}(Q_{1})
.
Suppose
\mathbb{R}^{\prime},\mathbb{R}^{\prime\prime}
are the metachords in
(\mathcal{L}^{0}\cup\mathcal{L}^{1})_{0}

such that
\{\mathbb{R}^{\prime},\mathbb{R}^{\prime\prime}\}=\{\mathbb{R}^{0},\mathbb{R}^{1}\}
for some
R\in\mathcal{S}(Q_{2})
and
\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{R}^{\prime}),\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{R}^{\prime\prime})

are at the same positions in
\mathsf{canon}(Q_{2},\pi(Q_{2}))

as
\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{S}^{0}),\mathsf{num}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
in
\mathsf{canon}(Q_{1},\pi(Q_{1}))
.
Clearly, such
\mathbb{R}^{\prime},\mathbb{R}^{\prime\prime}
exists as
\mathsf{canon}(Q_{1},\pi(Q_{1}))=\mathsf{canon}(Q_{2},\pi(Q_{2}))
.
In particular, it means that
\mathbb{S}^{0}
and
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}
are locally isomorphic.
Suppose
\alpha_{S}:S\to R
establishes a local isomorphism between
\mathbb{S}^{0}
and
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}
.
Now, we can easily check that the mapping

\alpha=\bigcup\{\alpha_{P}:P\in N_{\mathbb{T}}(Q_{1})\smallsetminus\{P_{1}\}\}\cup\bigcup\{\alpha_{S}:S\in\mathcal{S}(Q_{1})\}

establishes a local isomorphism between
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
.
∎

Suppose
P
is a P-node in
\mathbb{T}
.
We arrange the children of
P
in
\mathbb{T}
into a sequence
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{k}

such that
\mathsf{num}(Q_{i})\leqslant\mathsf{num}(Q_{j})
for every
i<j
.
We set

\mathsf{canon}(P)=(\mathsf{num}(Q_{1}),\ldots,\mathsf{num}(Q_{k})).

Clearly, by Proposition
11.5
, we can compute
\mathsf{canon}(P)
for all P-nodes

P\in N(\mathbb{T})_{l}
in linear time in the size of
N(\mathbb{T})_{l+1}
.

Claim 11.10
.

Suppose
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are two nodes such that
\mathsf{level}(P_{1})=\mathsf{level}(P_{2})
with the parents
Q_{1},Q_{2}
in
\mathbb{T}
.
Then,
P_{1}
is locally isomorphic to
P_{2}
if and only if
\mathsf{canon}(P_{1})=\mathsf{canon}(P_{2})
.

Proof.

Suppose
\alpha
is a local isomorphism between
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
.
Note the
\alpha
maps a child
Q^{\prime}
of
P_{1}
into a child
Q^{\prime\prime}
of
P_{2}
and
the restriction of
\alpha
to
V_{\mathbb{T}}(Q^{\prime})
establishes a local isomorphism between
Q^{\prime}
and
Q^{\prime\prime}
(and hence
\mathsf{num}(Q^{\prime})=\mathsf{num}(Q^{\prime\prime})
).
Suppose
Q^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,Q^{\prime}_{k}
are the children of
P_{1}
in
\mathbb{T}
and

Q^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,Q^{\prime\prime}_{k}
are the children of
P_{2}
in
\mathbb{T}
such that
\alpha
restricted to
V_{\mathbb{T}}(Q^{\prime}_{i})
is a local isomorphism between
Q^{\prime}_{i}
and
Q^{\prime\prime}_{i}
.
So, we have
\mathsf{num}(Q^{\prime}_{i})=\mathsf{num}(Q^{\prime\prime}_{i})
for every
i\in[k]
, and hence
\mathsf{canon}(P_{1})=\mathsf{canon}(P_{2})
.

Suppose
\mathsf{canon}(P_{1})=\mathsf{canon}(P_{2})
.
Suppose
\mathsf{canon}(P_{1})=(\mathsf{num}(Q^{\prime}_{1}),\ldots,\mathsf{num}(Q^{\prime}_{k}))
and
\mathsf{canon}(P_{2})=(\mathsf{num}(Q^{\prime\prime}_{1}),\ldots,\mathsf{num}(Q^{\prime\prime}_{k}))
, where

Q^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,Q^{\prime}_{k}
and
Q^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,Q^{\prime\prime}_{k}
are the children of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
, respectively, in
\mathbb{T}
.
Since
\mathsf{canon}(P_{1})=\mathsf{canon}(P_{2})
, we have
\mathsf{num}(Q^{\prime}_{i})=\mathsf{num}(Q^{\prime\prime}_{i})
for every
i\in[k]
.
It means that
Q^{\prime}_{i}
and
Q^{\prime\prime}_{i}
are locally isomorphic.
Suppose that
\alpha_{i}:V_{\mathbb{T}^{*}}(Q^{\prime}_{i})\to V_{\mathbb{T}^{*}}(Q^{\prime\prime}_{i})
is a local isomorphism between
Q^{\prime}_{i}
and
Q^{\prime\prime}_{i}
for
i\in[k]
.
Now, one can easily check that the mapping
\alpha=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\alpha_{i}

establishes a local isomorphism between
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
.
∎

Eventually, suppose
\mathbb{T}^{PQ}
is bicentered and
R
is the root of
\mathbb{T}^{PQ}
.
In this case we set

\mathsf{canon}(R)=(\mathsf{num}(P),\mathsf{num}(Q)),

where
P
and
Q
are the children of
R
.

Summing up, the observations made above assert that we can compute
\mathsf{canon}(N)
and
\mathsf{num}(N)
for all nodes in
N(\mathbb{T})_{l}
in time linear in the size of

|N(\mathbb{T})_{l,l+1}|+\sum\big\{|S(Q)|:Q\text{ is a Q-node in }N(\mathbb{T})_{l}\big\}.

So, the second step of the canonization procedure can be performed in linear time in the size of
G
.
Eventually, we set

\mathsf{canon}(G,m_{G},u_{G})=(u_{G},Num-1,\mathsf{canon}(Num-1),\ldots,2,\mathsf{canon}(2),1,\mathsf{canon}(1))

and

\mathsf{canon}(G^{\prime})=\mathsf{canon}(G,u_{G},m_{G}).

As we have argued,
\mathsf{canon}(G^{\prime})
can be computed in time
O(n)
, where
n=|V(G^{\prime})|
.
Note that the size of
\mathsf{canon}(G^{\prime})
is linear in
n
,
and each entry in
\mathsf{canon}(G^{\prime})
is a natural number in the range
O(n)
.
So, to prove Theorem
1.2
it remains to show

G^{\prime}\text{ and }H^{\prime}\text{ are isomorphic if and only if }\mathsf{canon}(G^{\prime})=\mathsf{canon}(H^{\prime})

for every two circular-arc graphs
G^{\prime}
and
H^{\prime}
.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Suppose
G^{\prime}
and
H^{\prime}
are isomorphic circular-arc graphs,
represented by
(G,m_{G},u_{G})
and
(H,m_{H},u_{H})
, respectively.
Suppose
\alpha
establishes an isomorphism between
(G,m_{G},u_{G})
and
(H,m_{H},u_{H})
.
Hence,
\alpha
establishes a local isomorphism between the root of the PQS-tree
\mathbb{T}_{G}

of
G
and the root of the PQS-tree
\mathbb{T}_{H}
of
H
.
Since
\alpha
establishes a local isomorphism between the metachords of
G
and of
H
,
and between the nodes of
\mathbb{T}_{G}
and
\mathbb{T}_{H}
,
the tables
\mathsf{canon}_{G^{\prime}}
and
\mathsf{canon}_{H^{\prime}}
computed for
G^{\prime}
and
H^{\prime}
contain
the tuples with the same entries.
Hence, we have
\mathsf{canon}(G,m_{G},u_{G})=\mathsf{canon}(G,m_{H},u_{H})
.

Suppose that
\mathsf{canon}(G,m_{G},u_{G})=\mathsf{canon}(G,m_{H},u_{H})
.
To show that
(G,m_{G},u_{G})
and
(H,m_{H},u_{H})
are isomorphic,
we traverse the tables
\mathsf{canon}_{G}
and
\mathsf{canon}_{H}
from the first to the last entry and we prove,
as in Claim
11.4
and Claims
11.9
–
11.10
, that the tuples
stored at the same position in tables
\mathsf{canon}_{G}
and
\mathsf{canon}_{H}
encode the objects (metachords or nodes of the PQS-trees) of
G
and of
H
that are locally isomorphic.
In particular, the roots of
\mathbb{T}_{G}
and of
\mathbb{T}_{H}
are locally isomorphic,
which shows that
(G,m_{G},u_{G})
and
(H,m_{H},u_{H})
, and hence
G^{\prime}
and
H^{\prime}
, are isomorphic.
∎

12.
